Well, what brings me in is... I guess it's this memorial I'm putting together for my brother. It’s just gotten so much bigger than I originally meant it to be. I’ve been spending most of my days, you know, chasing down the perfect songs, calling up folks we haven't seen in decades, planning these elaborate displays... and trips, too! We have to go back to our hometown for some of it. Which reminds me, the old diner we used to eat at there, it closed down last year. A real shame, they had the best cherry pie. I used to go there every Sunday after church. But anyway, my wife, she’s the one who made the appointment. She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?

Well, I definitely want to do it. He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. You remember the glue they used to sell for those kits? It had this incredibly strong smell. I'm pretty sure they banned it back in the 80s because teenagers were sniffing it or something. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. She was absolutely furious, out there on the porch waving her broom around at him. 

But, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memorial. Yeah, it's not that anyone is forcing me to do it. I just want it to be absolutely perfect, and I feel like I'm the only one who can really see the whole vision for it.

And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...

My wife says our days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from another call, trip, or argument.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, ever since my brother passed and I began working on his tribute.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I guess it's this memorial I'm putting together for my brother. It’s just gotten so much bigger than I originally meant it to be. I’ve been spending most of my days, you know, chasing down the perfect songs, calling up folks we haven't seen in decades, planning these elaborate displays... and trips, too! We have to go back to our hometown for some of it. Which reminds me, the old diner we used to eat at there, it closed down last year. A real shame, they had the best cherry pie. I used to go there every Sunday after church. But anyway, my wife, she’s the one who made the appointment. She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do all these things for the memorial, or is it something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Well, I definitely want to do it. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. She was absolutely furious, out there on the porch waving her broom around at him. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memorial. Yeah, it's not that anyone is forcing me to do it. I just want it to be absolutely perfect, and I feel like I'm the only one who can really see the whole vision for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the memorial even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. I'll sit down to eat my oatmeal, and my mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next. One minute it’s figuring out the dimensions for a display case for his old baseball trophies, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to track down a second cousin, then a verse from an old ballad we used to sing just pops in... and I can’t slow my brain down enough to write any of it down in order! \n\nI really need to get it on paper, especially the part about Cousin Arthur. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. I went down to the lumber yard to look at some oak for that display case, and I nearly fell over when I saw the price tag. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the things you're trying to do for the memorial?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, overwhelmed? No, no, I wouldn't say overwhelmed. I mean, there's a lot to do, sure, but it's energizing, you know? It's like I finally have a real purpose right now. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... 

But, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.

He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. But anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.

He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. 

But, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. 

But I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.

I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!

He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. 

My brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... 

But anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.

I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. 

It's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.

I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus on the memorial is affecting your relationship with your wife in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. \n\nBut I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the memorial if you want to?\n\nPatient: Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. \n\nIt's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. 

Speaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... 

But no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.

I'll sit down to eat my oatmeal, and my mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next. One minute it’s figuring out the dimensions for a display case for his old baseball trophies, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to track down a second cousin, then a verse from an old ballad we used to sing just pops in... and I can’t slow my brain down enough to write any of it down in order! \n\nI really need to get it on paper, especially the part about Cousin Arthur. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. I went down to the lumber yard to look at some oak for that display case, and I nearly fell over when I saw the price tag. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the things you're trying to do for the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, overwhelmed? No, no, I wouldn't say overwhelmed. I mean, there's a lot to do, sure, but it's energizing, you know? It's like I finally have a real purpose right now. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus on the memorial is affecting your relationship with your wife in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. \n\nBut I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the memorial if you want to?\n\nPatient: Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. \n\nIt's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you're misinterpreting the meanings in those old ballads?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

This is a key feature of F23.0, acute transient psychotic disorder, which involves the sudden onset of psychotic symptoms, such as delusions, that are not better explained by another condition.", "is_final": false}

My wife says our days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from another call, trip, or argument.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, ever since my brother passed and I began working on his tribute.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. \n\nIt's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you're misinterpreting the meanings in those old ballads?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the memorial is more important than your relationships with your wife and friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, I... I wouldn't say it's more important, but it's just what has to be done right now. My wife, she's really frustrated with me. She says our entire days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from making another phone call, or taking another trip to the hardware store, or getting into another argument about the memorial display. It's just... there's so much to do, you know? 

I've even missed my walking group the last few weeks. We usually walk around the reservoir every Tuesday morning. It's a nice group, mostly folks from the neighborhood. We always stop at that little bakery on the corner of Elm and Main afterwards. They make these bear claws that are just incredible—flaky pastry, real almond paste inside, and sliced almonds on top. Not like the ones you get at the supermarket that are just dry bread with sugar glaze. You really have to get there before 9:00 AM though, or they sell out. The baker, his name is Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He used to be a boxer in his youth, can you believe it? Got a nose that looks like it's been broken three times, but he makes the most delicate pastries. 

Anyway, I haven't been to the walking group, and I haven't been to my book club either. I haven't even touched the garden. The weeds are probably taking over the tomatoes by now. I've even managed to upset some of my friends over at the senior center because, well, I guess everything has become about the memorial display lately. But I just can't focus on anything else until this is finished. It's too important.

She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do all these things for the memorial, or is it something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Well, I definitely want to do it. He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. You remember the glue they used to sell for those kits? It had this incredibly strong smell. I'm pretty sure they banned it back in the 80s because teenagers were sniffing it or something. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. One minute it’s figuring out the dimensions for a display case for his old baseball trophies, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to track down a second cousin, then a verse from an old ballad we used to sing just pops in... and I can’t slow my brain down enough to write any of it down in order! \n\nI really need to get it on paper, especially the part about Cousin Arthur. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. I went down to the lumber yard to look at some oak for that display case, and I nearly fell over when I saw the price tag. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the things you're trying to do for the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, overwhelmed? No, no, I wouldn't say overwhelmed. I mean, there's a lot to do, sure, but it's energizing, you know? It's like I finally have a real purpose right now. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memorial is more important than your relationships with your wife and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say it's more important, but it's just what has to be done right now. My wife, she's really frustrated with me. She says our entire days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from making another phone call, or taking another trip to the hardware store, or getting into another argument about the memorial display. It's just... there's so much to do, you know? \n\nI've even missed my walking group the last few weeks. We usually walk around the reservoir every Tuesday morning. It's a nice group, mostly folks from the neighborhood. We always stop at that little bakery on the corner of Elm and Main afterwards. They make these bear claws that are just incredible—flaky pastry, real almond paste inside, and sliced almonds on top. Not like the ones you get at the supermarket that are just dry bread with sugar glaze. You really have to get there before 9:00 AM though, or they sell out. The baker, his name is Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He used to be a boxer in his youth, can you believe it? Got a nose that looks like it's been broken three times, but he makes the most delicate pastries. \n\nAnyway, I haven't been to the walking group, and I haven't been to my book club either. I haven't even touched the garden. The weeds are probably taking over the tomatoes by now. I've even managed to upset some of my friends over at the senior center because, well, I guess everything has become about the memorial display lately. But I just can't focus on anything else until this is finished. It's too important.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you're misinterpreting the meanings in those old ballads?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memorial is more important than your relationships with your wife and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say it's more important, but it's just what has to be done right now. My wife, she's really frustrated with me. She says our entire days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from making another phone call, or taking another trip to the hardware store, or getting into another argument about the memorial display. It's just... there's so much to do, you know? \n\nI've even missed my walking group the last few weeks. We usually walk around the reservoir every Tuesday morning. It's a nice group, mostly folks from the neighborhood. We always stop at that little bakery on the corner of Elm and Main afterwards. They make these bear claws that are just incredible—flaky pastry, real almond paste inside, and sliced almonds on top. Not like the ones you get at the supermarket that are just dry bread with sugar glaze. You really have to get there before 9:00 AM though, or they sell out. The baker, his name is Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He used to be a boxer in his youth, can you believe it? Got a nose that looks like it's been broken three times, but he makes the most delicate pastries. \n\nAnyway, I haven't been to the walking group, and I haven't been to my book club either. I haven't even touched the garden. The weeds are probably taking over the tomatoes by now. I've even managed to upset some of my friends over at the senior center because, well, I guess everything has become about the memorial display lately. But I just can't focus on anything else until this is finished. It's too important.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan anything convince you that the warnings you hear in the ballads might not be real or meant specifically for you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I still know what I heard in those words.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no. I mean, how could anything convince me otherwise? I know it sounds odd, I really do. I know my wife thinks I've lost the plot, she tells me I'm reading way too much into it. But I'm certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into them, just for me. I know what I heard in those words. 

My brother, he was always collecting things like that. Records, old stamps, coins. He had this whole album of wheat pennies. Do you remember those? They stopped making them in the fifties, I think. He had one from 1943 that was made of steel instead of copper because of the war. They needed the copper for ammunition, you see. It's fascinating, really, how war changes everything, even the money in your pocket. I remember my mother talking about ration books for sugar and butter. She used to save the bacon grease in a little tin can on the stove to use for cooking because you couldn't just go out and buy cooking oil whenever you wanted. People were so resourceful back then. Now, if the Wi-Fi goes out for ten minutes, people act like the world is ending. 

But no, to get back to what you were saying... nothing's going to convince me otherwise. I hear what I hear, and the warnings are real.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I guess it's this memorial I'm putting together for my brother. It’s just gotten so much bigger than I originally meant it to be. I’ve been spending most of my days, you know, chasing down the perfect songs, calling up folks we haven't seen in decades, planning these elaborate displays... and trips, too! We have to go back to our hometown for some of it. Which reminds me, the old diner we used to eat at there, it closed down last year. A real shame, they had the best cherry pie. I used to go there every Sunday after church. But anyway, my wife, she’s the one who made the appointment. She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do all these things for the memorial, or is it something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Well, I definitely want to do it. He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. You remember the glue they used to sell for those kits? It had this incredibly strong smell. I'm pretty sure they banned it back in the 80s because teenagers were sniffing it or something. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus on the memorial is affecting your relationship with your wife in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. \n\nBut I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the memorial if you want to?\n\nPatient: Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs.
