I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I'm certain those old ballads my brother kept aren't just songs. They've got warnings tucked in them specifically for me. So if someone else sat down and listened, they'd probably just hear the music. \n\nMy brother had this one record... it was a blues album, from the Delta. The vinyl was so thick and heavy, not like the flimsy ones they made in the eighties. You had to use a special needle for the 78s, you know. If you used the wrong needle, it would just tear the grooves right up. I remember going to the hardware store with my dad to buy replacement needles. The hardware store back then was amazing—it smelled like sawdust and motor oil, and they had these big wooden bins full of loose nails and screws. You didn't buy them in those little plastic blister packs like you do now. You'd scoop out what you needed, weigh them on a scale, and put them in a brown paper bag. The man who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he wore this heavy canvas apron with a hundred pockets. He always had a pencil tucked behind his ear. I try to keep a pencil behind my ear when I'm working on the memorial display, but it always falls out. Maybe my ears are the wrong shape for it. \n\nBut anyway, when my wife says I'm reading too much into the records and that anyone else would just hear regular singing, I just let her talk. Because I still know what I heard in those words. It's meant for me, not for anyone else who might put the needle on the record.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would happen to you if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, like I think I said a minute ago... I just don't know exactly what would happen, but it wouldn't be good. It's like ignoring a siren. You know, they used to test the civil defense sirens every first Tuesday of the month at noon where I grew up. Loudest thing you ever heard. We used to have to do those duck-and-cover drills in elementary school whenever they went off. We’d all crawl under our desks. We had these heavy oak desks—they still had the little holes for inkwells in the top, even though we all used ballpoint pens by then. Bic pens, usually, with the blue caps you could chew on. The teachers made us tuck our heads right down between our knees. Like a piece of wood was going to stop a nuclear bomb! My buddy Tommy, he used to try and tie my shoelaces together while we were huddled under there. He ended up moving to Chicago, became an accountant or something. I haven't seen him since 1982. \n\nBut anyway, ignoring a warning... you just don't do it. I know it sounds odd, I really do. And when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just let her complain, because I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. I still know what I heard in those words. So I just can't ignore them, whatever the consequences might be.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would be the worst thing that could happen if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, the absolute worst thing... I suppose the worst thing would be that I wouldn't be prepared for whatever is coming. Like I was saying, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, it's like when they tell you a storm is coming on the radio. My father used to have this big Zenith trans-oceanic shortwave radio. It had this incredible dial with all the world cities listed on it—London, Paris, Tokyo. He’d sit up late at night twisting that big tuning knob, trying to catch a signal bouncing off the ionosphere. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ve been spending most of my days, you know, chasing down the perfect songs, calling up folks we haven't seen in decades, planning these elaborate displays... and trips, too! We have to go back to our hometown for some of it. Which reminds me, the old diner we used to eat at there, it closed down last year. A real shame, they had the best cherry pie. I used to go there every Sunday after church. But anyway, my wife, she’s the one who made the appointment. She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do all these things for the memorial, or is it something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Well, I definitely want to do it. He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. You remember the glue they used to sell for those kits? It had this incredibly strong smell. I'm pretty sure they banned it back in the 80s because teenagers were sniffing it or something. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. She was absolutely furious, out there on the porch waving her broom around at him. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memorial. Yeah, it's not that anyone is forcing me to do it. I just want it to be absolutely perfect, and I feel like I'm the only one who can really see the whole vision for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the memorial even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. I'll sit down to eat my oatmeal, and my mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next. One minute it’s figuring out the dimensions for a display case for his old baseball trophies, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to track down a second cousin, then a verse from an old ballad we used to sing just pops in... and I can’t slow my brain down enough to write any of it down in order! \n\nI really need to get it on paper, especially the part about Cousin Arthur. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. I went down to the lumber yard to look at some oak for that display case, and I nearly fell over when I saw the price tag. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the things you're trying to do for the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, overwhelmed? No, no, I wouldn't say overwhelmed. I mean, there's a lot to do, sure, but it's energizing, you know? It's like I finally have a real purpose right now. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus on the memorial is affecting your relationship with your wife in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. \n\nBut I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the memorial if you want to?\n\nPatient: Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. \n\nIt's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you're misinterpreting the meanings in those old ballads?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memorial is more important than your relationships with your wife and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say it's more important, but it's just what has to be done right now. My wife, she's really frustrated with me. She says our entire days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from making another phone call, or taking another trip to the hardware store, or getting into another argument about the memorial display. It's just... there's so much to do, you know? \n\nI've even missed my walking group the last few weeks. We usually walk around the reservoir every Tuesday morning. It's a nice group, mostly folks from the neighborhood. We always stop at that little bakery on the corner of Elm and Main afterwards. They make these bear claws that are just incredible—flaky pastry, real almond paste inside, and sliced almonds on top. Not like the ones you get at the supermarket that are just dry bread with sugar glaze. You really have to get there before 9:00 AM though, or they sell out. The baker, his name is Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He used to be a boxer in his youth, can you believe it? Got a nose that looks like it's been broken three times, but he makes the most delicate pastries. \n\nAnyway, I haven't been to the walking group, and I haven't been to my book club either. I haven't even touched the garden. The weeds are probably taking over the tomatoes by now. I've even managed to upset some of my friends over at the senior center because, well, I guess everything has become about the memorial display lately. But I just can't focus on anything else until this is finished. It's too important.\n\nDoctor: Can anything convince you that the warnings you hear in the ballads might not be real or meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no. I mean, how could anything convince me otherwise? I know it sounds odd, I really do. I know my wife thinks I've lost the plot, she tells me I'm reading way too much into it. But I'm certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into them, just for me. I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he was always collecting things like that. Records, old stamps, coins. He had this whole album of wheat pennies. Do you remember those? They stopped making them in the fifties, I think. He had one from 1943 that was made of steel instead of copper because of the war. They needed the copper for ammunition, you see. It's fascinating, really, how war changes everything, even the money in your pocket. I remember my mother talking about ration books for sugar and butter. She used to save the bacon grease in a little tin can on the stove to use for cooking because you couldn't just go out and buy cooking oil whenever you wanted. People were so resourceful back then. Now, if the Wi-Fi goes out for ten minutes, people act like the world is ending. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were saying... nothing's going to convince me otherwise. I hear what I hear, and the warnings are real.\n\nDoctor: What do you think will happen if you don't follow the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, if I don't listen to them... I don't even want to find out. I know I keep repeating myself, and I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and I can't just ignore a warning like that. \n\nMy brother, he took such good care of those records. He used to play them on this massive wooden console stereo. It was a beautiful piece of furniture, really, took up half the living room wall. It had a radio built right into it, too, with those warm vacuum tubes that took a minute to heat up before the sound would come through. We used to sit around it and listen to the baseball games on Sunday afternoons. The announcers back then, they had such smooth voices. You could just close your eyes and perfectly picture the green grass of the outfield. I went to a game at Fenway Park once, back in... oh, it must have been '78 or '79. We had hot dogs that actually snapped when you bit into them. Best hot dog I ever had. They put celery salt on them, which sounds strange, but it really brings out the flavor. I actually bought a little bottle of celery salt at the grocer last week, but my wife hid it in the back of the pantry because she says it's too much sodium for my blood pressure. \n\nShe's always getting on my case about something these days. Just like when my wife says I’m reading too much into it with the records. She just doesn't get it. But I still know what I heard in those words. So, to answer you, I just can't risk finding out what happens if I don't listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else could hear these warnings if they listened to the ballads in the same way you do?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they could. I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I'm certain those old ballads my brother kept aren't just songs. They've got warnings tucked in them specifically for me. So if someone else sat down and listened, they'd probably just hear the music. \n\nMy brother had this one record... it was a blues album, from the Delta. The vinyl was so thick and heavy, not like the flimsy ones they made in the eighties. You had to use a special needle for the 78s, you know. If you used the wrong needle, it would just tear the grooves right up. I remember going to the hardware store with my dad to buy replacement needles. The hardware store back then was amazing—it smelled like sawdust and motor oil, and they had these big wooden bins full of loose nails and screws. You didn't buy them in those little plastic blister packs like you do now. You'd scoop out what you needed, weigh them on a scale, and put them in a brown paper bag. The man who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he wore this heavy canvas apron with a hundred pockets. He always had a pencil tucked behind his ear. I try to keep a pencil behind my ear when I'm working on the memorial display, but it always falls out. Maybe my ears are the wrong shape for it. \n\nBut anyway, when my wife says I'm reading too much into the records and that anyone else would just hear regular singing, I just let her talk. Because I still know what I heard in those words. It's meant for me, not for anyone else who might put the needle on the record.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would happen to you if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, like I think I said a minute ago... I just don't know exactly what would happen, but it wouldn't be good. It's like ignoring a siren. You know, they used to test the civil defense sirens every first Tuesday of the month at noon where I grew up. Loudest thing you ever heard. We used to have to do those duck-and-cover drills in elementary school whenever they went off. We’d all crawl under our desks. We had these heavy oak desks—they still had the little holes for inkwells in the top, even though we all used ballpoint pens by then. Bic pens, usually, with the blue caps you could chew on. The teachers made us tuck our heads right down between our knees. Like a piece of wood was going to stop a nuclear bomb! My buddy Tommy, he used to try and tie my shoelaces together while we were huddled under there. He ended up moving to Chicago, became an accountant or something. I haven't seen him since 1982. \n\nBut anyway, ignoring a warning... you just don't do it. I know it sounds odd, I really do. And when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just let her complain, because I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. I still know what I heard in those words. So I just can't ignore them, whatever the consequences might be.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would be the worst thing that could happen if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, the absolute worst thing... I suppose the worst thing would be that I wouldn't be prepared for whatever is coming. Like I was saying, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, it's like when they tell you a storm is coming on the radio. My father used to have this big Zenith trans-oceanic shortwave radio. It had this incredible dial with all the world cities listed on it—London, Paris, Tokyo. He’d sit up late at night twisting that big tuning knob, trying to catch a signal bouncing off the ionosphere. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think it's possible that the warnings you hear in the ballads are just a product of your own mind, rather than actual messages meant for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Look, Doc, I know I keep saying this, and I know exactly how odd it sounds to you. My wife tells me the exact same thing you're asking—she says it's just my mind playing tricks, that I’m reading way too much into it. But I’m absolutely certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just regular songs. There are warnings tucked right in there for me. I still know what I heard in those words, and it's not my imagination. 

Speaking of imagination, my grandfather used to have the wildest imagination of anyone I ever met. We used to sit out on his front porch in the evenings. He had this heavy wooden rocking chair that squeaked every single time he leaned back. He'd smoke a pipe, usually a cherry blend tobacco. You could smell that sweet cherry smoke halfway down the block before you even got to his house. Anyway, he used to tell us these tall tales, swore up and down he fought a black bear with his bare hands up in the UP—the Upper Peninsula, in Michigan. We believed him for years, right up until my grandmother finally told us he'd never even been north of Saginaw. She used to make the best cherry pie, my grandmother. She picked the tart cherries right off the tree in their side yard. You had to be careful eating it, though, because she'd always miss a few pits. You had to spit them out into a little paper napkin. 

But no, to answer your question... it's not a product of my mind. I know what I heard in those records.

It had a radio built right into it, too, with those warm vacuum tubes that took a minute to heat up before the sound would come through. We used to sit around it and listen to the baseball games on Sunday afternoons. The announcers back then, they had such smooth voices. You could just close your eyes and perfectly picture the green grass of the outfield. I went to a game at Fenway Park once, back in... oh, it must have been '78 or '79. We had hot dogs that actually snapped when you bit into them. Best hot dog I ever had. They put celery salt on them, which sounds strange, but it really brings out the flavor. I actually bought a little bottle of celery salt at the grocer last week, but my wife hid it in the back of the pantry because she says it's too much sodium for my blood pressure. \n\nShe's always getting on my case about something these days. Just like when my wife says I’m reading too much into it with the records. She just doesn't get it. But I still know what I heard in those words. So, to answer you, I just can't risk finding out what happens if I don't listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else could hear these warnings if they listened to the ballads in the same way you do?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they could. I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I'm certain those old ballads my brother kept aren't just songs. They've got warnings tucked in them specifically for me. So if someone else sat down and listened, they'd probably just hear the music. \n\nMy brother had this one record... it was a blues album, from the Delta. The vinyl was so thick and heavy, not like the flimsy ones they made in the eighties. You had to use a special needle for the 78s, you know. If you used the wrong needle, it would just tear the grooves right up. I remember going to the hardware store with my dad to buy replacement needles. The hardware store back then was amazing—it smelled like sawdust and motor oil, and they had these big wooden bins full of loose nails and screws. You didn't buy them in those little plastic blister packs like you do now. You'd scoop out what you needed, weigh them on a scale, and put them in a brown paper bag. The man who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he wore this heavy canvas apron with a hundred pockets. He always had a pencil tucked behind his ear. I try to keep a pencil behind my ear when I'm working on the memorial display, but it always falls out. Maybe my ears are the wrong shape for it. \n\nBut anyway, when my wife says I'm reading too much into the records and that anyone else would just hear regular singing, I just let her talk. Because I still know what I heard in those words. It's meant for me, not for anyone else who might put the needle on the record.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would happen to you if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, like I think I said a minute ago... I just don't know exactly what would happen, but it wouldn't be good. It's like ignoring a siren. You know, they used to test the civil defense sirens every first Tuesday of the month at noon where I grew up. Loudest thing you ever heard. We used to have to do those duck-and-cover drills in elementary school whenever they went off. We’d all crawl under our desks. We had these heavy oak desks—they still had the little holes for inkwells in the top, even though we all used ballpoint pens by then. Bic pens, usually, with the blue caps you could chew on. The teachers made us tuck our heads right down between our knees. Like a piece of wood was going to stop a nuclear bomb! My buddy Tommy, he used to try and tie my shoelaces together while we were huddled under there. He ended up moving to Chicago, became an accountant or something. I haven't seen him since 1982. \n\nBut anyway, ignoring a warning... you just don't do it. I know it sounds odd, I really do. And when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just let her complain, because I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. I still know what I heard in those words. So I just can't ignore them, whatever the consequences might be.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would be the worst thing that could happen if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, the absolute worst thing... I suppose the worst thing would be that I wouldn't be prepared for whatever is coming. Like I was saying, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, it's like when they tell you a storm is coming on the radio. My father used to have this big Zenith trans-oceanic shortwave radio. It had this incredible dial with all the world cities listed on it—London, Paris, Tokyo. He’d sit up late at night twisting that big tuning knob, trying to catch a signal bouncing off the ionosphere. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the warnings you hear in the ballads are just a product of your own mind, rather than actual messages meant for you?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I know I keep saying this, and I know exactly how odd it sounds to you. My wife tells me the exact same thing you're asking—she says it's just my mind playing tricks, that I’m reading way too much into it. But I’m absolutely certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just regular songs. There are warnings tucked right in there for me. I still know what I heard in those words, and it's not my imagination. \n\nSpeaking of imagination, my grandfather used to have the wildest imagination of anyone I ever met. We used to sit out on his front porch in the evenings. He had this heavy wooden rocking chair that squeaked every single time he leaned back. He'd smoke a pipe, usually a cherry blend tobacco. You could smell that sweet cherry smoke halfway down the block before you even got to his house. Anyway, he used to tell us these tall tales, swore up and down he fought a black bear with his bare hands up in the UP—the Upper Peninsula, in Michigan. We believed him for years, right up until my grandmother finally told us he'd never even been north of Saginaw. She used to make the best cherry pie, my grandmother. She picked the tart cherries right off the tree in their side yard. You had to be careful eating it, though, because she'd always miss a few pits. You had to spit them out into a little paper napkin. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... it's not a product of my mind. I know what I heard in those records.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, it's like when they tell you a storm is coming on the radio. My father used to have this big Zenith trans-oceanic shortwave radio. It had this incredible dial with all the world cities listed on it—London, Paris, Tokyo. He’d sit up late at night twisting that big tuning knob, trying to catch a signal bouncing off the ionosphere. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the warnings you hear in the ballads are just a product of your own mind, rather than actual messages meant for you?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I know I keep saying this, and I know exactly how odd it sounds to you. I still know what I heard in those words, and it's not my imagination. \n\nSpeaking of imagination, my grandfather used to have the wildest imagination of anyone I ever met. She picked the tart cherries right off the tree in their side yard. You had to be careful eating it, though, because she'd always miss a few pits. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, even if a psychiatrist told me they aren't there... I mean, no offense to you, Doc, but I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. 

You know, psychiatrists... my neighbor, Phil, he went to a psychiatrist once back in the seventies. He had this terrible fear of driving over bridges. Amaxophobia, I think they call it, or maybe gephyrophobia. Anyway, Phil drove this beautiful 1969 Chevy Impala. It was a two-door hardtop, cherry red with a white vinyl roof. He kept it immaculate. Every Sunday morning, he'd be out in the driveway with a bucket of soapy water and a chamois cloth. He even polished the chrome bumpers until you could see your reflection in them. But if he had to cross the river to get to the hardware store on the other side of town, he'd drive ten miles out of his way just to take the road that went around the water instead of over the bridge. The bridge was one of those old steel truss ones, painted a faded green, and it rattled whenever a heavy truck went over it. The city finally tore it down a few years ago and put up a concrete one. It's much quieter now, but it doesn't have the same character. 

But like I was saying... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, or if a doctor tells me it's not real, I just... I still know what I heard in those words. I can't just unhear it because someone else tells me I should.

We have to go back to our hometown for some of it. Which reminds me, the old diner we used to eat at there, it closed down last year. A real shame, they had the best cherry pie. I used to go there every Sunday after church. But anyway, my wife, she’s the one who made the appointment. She says I’ve completely pushed aside my garden—which is true, the tomatoes are looking a bit sad—and I haven't been to my walking group in weeks, and... well, just about everything else. I just have so much to do for the memorial, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do all these things for the memorial, or is it something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Well, I definitely want to do it. He's my brother, you know? He deserves a proper send-off. We used to build these model airplanes together when we were kids... mostly B-17 bombers and Mustangs. You remember the glue they used to sell for those kits? It had this incredibly strong smell. I'm pretty sure they banned it back in the 80s because teenagers were sniffing it or something. Kids really do the darnedest things, don't they? Just last week, my neighbor's grandson was over, riding his little plastic tricycle right through my wife's petunias. She was absolutely furious, out there on the porch waving her broom around at him. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memorial. Yeah, it's not that anyone is forcing me to do it. I just want it to be absolutely perfect, and I feel like I'm the only one who can really see the whole vision for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the memorial even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. I'll sit down to eat my oatmeal, and my mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next. One minute it’s figuring out the dimensions for a display case for his old baseball trophies, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to track down a second cousin, then a verse from an old ballad we used to sing just pops in... and I can’t slow my brain down enough to write any of it down in order! \n\nI really need to get it on paper, especially the part about Cousin Arthur. He moved out to Cleveland back in, oh, must have been '98? Bought a little hardware store out there. You know, speaking of hardware, the price of lumber right now is just absolute highway robbery. I went down to the lumber yard to look at some oak for that display case, and I nearly fell over when I saw the price tag. And the kid working there—couldn't have been more than nineteen, had this silly haircut shaved all up the sides—he didn't know a single thing about wood grains. I asked him about the moisture content and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today just don't have the same work ethic, do they? My first job was delivering papers at five in the morning, rain or shine...\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or stressed by all the things you're trying to do for the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, overwhelmed? No, no, I wouldn't say overwhelmed. I mean, there's a lot to do, sure, but it's energizing, you know? It's like I finally have a real purpose right now. Though, I suppose my wife would say I'm stressed. She keeps telling me I need to sit down and rest my heart. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he’s been telling me to watch my blood pressure for years. He’s a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these loud bow ties. I asked him once where he gets them, and he said his daughter makes them for him. She’s got a little business on the internet, selling them. The internet is just amazing, isn't it? You can buy anything on there now. My neighbor, Frank, he bought a whole tractor engine online. Just had it shipped right to his driveway in a wooden crate. Took three men to move it into his garage. We spent all of last summer trying to get that thing running... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't feel stressed about the memorial. I just have to get it done, and I'm the only one who can do it right.\n\nDoctor: How has your relationship with your wife been since you started planning the memorial?\n\nPatient: Well, she says... she says our whole life right now just revolves around calming me down, or, you know, trying to stop me from making another phone call or planning another trip. Or getting into another argument about the memorial. She thinks I'm letting everything else go. And I guess... I guess I have missed my walking group the last few weeks. And my book club. I just haven't had the time! \n\nOh, and the garden is an absolute mess right now, which is a shame because the tomatoes were just starting to come in. You know, there’s a real trick to growing good tomatoes. You’ve got to put crushed eggshells in the soil for the calcium, prevents that blossom end rot. My grandfather taught me that. He had this massive garden out back of his place in Pennsylvania. Grew everything—corn, beans, squash. He used to say you could live off the land if you just knew how to listen to it. He was a tough old bird, worked in the coal mines for forty years... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I’ve even upset some of my friends down at the senior center because, well, I guess every time I go down there, all I can talk about is the memorial display. But it's important! It has to be right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus on the memorial is affecting your relationship with your wife in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I suppose it is... negative to her, anyway. She tells me our whole day is just her trying to talk me off a ledge, you know, trying to calm me down or stop me from making another phone call or booking a trip. I wanted to fly out to Seattle to check on a bronze plaque maker, and she just about lost her mind. Seattle is beautiful, though. Rains a lot, but the coffee! I had a cup of coffee there back in '82 that I still think about. It was at this little diner near that market... Pike Place, where they throw the fish. Have you ever seen them throw the giant fish right over the counter? It's a real spectacle. The guys working there, they must have forearms like Popeye to be tossing those things around all day. I always wondered if they practice with rubber fish after hours. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Right, my wife. Yeah, she's upset. And it's not just her, honestly. Like I mentioned, I’ve completely let the garden go to weeds, missed my book club... I haven't even been to my walking group in weeks. I used to walk three miles every Tuesday. Good for the joints. I have those special orthopedic shoes, the white ones with the thick soles? Cost me a hundred and twenty dollars at the podiatrist's office. Medicare wouldn't cover a dime of it, which is a whole other scam if you ask me. \n\nBut I just don't have the time to walk right now! And the fellas down at the senior center are annoyed with me too, because, well, everything has just become about the memorial display when I'm around them. I can't help it if it's the most important thing going on right now! So, yes, I guess you could say it's causing some friction with everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the memorial if you want to?\n\nPatient: Stop thinking about it? No, I don't think I could even if I tried. My mind just keeps jumping from one idea to the next, like a ping-pong ball. One minute I'm thinking about the dimensions of the display case, then suddenly I'm planning a road trip to find this second cousin who might have an old photograph, and then I'm trying to remember a verse from a ballad we used to sing... I can’t slow it down enough to even write it all in order. I bought one of those nice leather-bound notebooks to keep track of everything. You know the ones? The paper has that thick, creamy feel to it. They smell like a saddle shop. I used to go to this saddle shop when I was a kid, over in the next county. Old man Henderson ran it. He had hands like catcher's mitts, thick and calloused from working the leather all day. He used to give me these little scraps of rawhide to chew on. People don't make things by hand like that anymore, do they? It's all machines and plastic now. Even the notebooks, most of them fall apart if you look at them funny. But this leather one, I thought it would help me organize my thoughts, but the pages are just a mess of scribbles because I can't write fast enough to keep up with my own brain!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things for the memorial because you have to, or because you genuinely want to?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s not really a matter of just wanting to. I mean, I have to do it. I know it probably sounds odd, and my wife tells me I’m reading way too much into things, but... I am certain that those old ballads my brother kept, they aren’t just songs. They’ve got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. She can say what she wants, but I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he had hundreds of those old records. Kept them in these heavy wooden crates that always smelled like dust and dried pine. We used to haul them around in the back of his old Chevy pickup. Lord, that truck was a beauty. Two-tone, robin's egg blue and white. You just don't see vehicles painted like that anymore, do you? You look out on the highway today and it’s just a sea of gray and silver and black. It’s like everyone is terrified of a little color! I remember we drove that Chevy all the way down to Nashville back in '74. Or maybe it was '75... no, '74, because that was the year the transmission blew out on us right outside of Louisville. We had to sit at this greasy spoon diner for two whole days just waiting on a part from the mechanic down the road. Best biscuits and gravy I ever had in my life, though. The waitress had this massive blonde beehive hairdo, must have used a whole can of hairspray on it every morning... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know what I heard in those songs. So I don't really have a choice in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the song lyrics if they listened to them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they would. That's the thing. My wife, she listened to a couple of them with me, just to humor me, I think, and she said it was just... just old folk music. She says I'm reading way too much into it. But I know what I heard. It's like... it's like the words were written just for me, you know? The warnings are tucked right in there, plain as day, but only if you're the one meant to hear them. \n\nIt's funny how hearing works, isn't it? Like my neighbor, Tom. He's got these hearing aids, cost him an arm and a leg. He got them at that place over in the strip mall next to the Chinese restaurant. Have you ever eaten there? Golden Dragon, I think it's called. They make this sweet and sour pork that is just out of this world. The pork is so crispy, and the sauce isn't too sweet, just the right amount of tang. They serve it with those little crispy noodles in the plastic bag before the meal. I could eat a whole bowl of just those noodles with a little duck sauce. Anyway, Tom, he got these hearing aids, and he says he can hear a pin drop in the next room now, but he still can't hear his wife when she tells him to take out the trash! Selective hearing, she calls it. I guess we all hear what we want to hear, or what we're supposed to hear. But with the ballads, it's not selective hearing. I'm certain of it. I know it sounds odd to other people, but the messages are real, and they're for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you're misinterpreting the meanings in those old ballads?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I'm misinterpreting them at all. I mean, I know it sounds odd. I know how it sounds to you, and I know how it sounds to my wife. She keeps telling me I'm reading way too much into it, that they're just old records. But I am certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into the lyrics, meant specifically for me. I know what I heard in those words, clear as a bell. \n\nSpeaking of bells, did you know my grandfather used to collect bells? He had this huge glass cabinet in his parlor, just filled with them. Brass bells, glass bells, little ceramic ones painted with flowers. He had this one big ship's bell that he'd ring every time we came over for Sunday dinner. It was so loud it would rattle your teeth! Sunday dinners were always a big production back then. My grandmother would make a roast, and there were always at least three different kinds of potatoes. Mashed, scalloped, and those little red ones roasted with rosemary. You can't find good rosemary at the grocery store anymore, it's always dried out and tastes like dust. I tried growing some in a pot on the back porch last summer, but the aphids got to it. Nasty little bugs. You have to spray them with soapy water, but even then, sometimes they just take over... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I'm not misinterpreting anything. The warnings are there, plain as day, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memorial is more important than your relationships with your wife and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say it's more important, but it's just what has to be done right now. My wife, she's really frustrated with me. She says our entire days revolve around calming me down or redirecting me from making another phone call, or taking another trip to the hardware store, or getting into another argument about the memorial display. It's just... there's so much to do, you know? \n\nI've even missed my walking group the last few weeks. We usually walk around the reservoir every Tuesday morning. It's a nice group, mostly folks from the neighborhood. We always stop at that little bakery on the corner of Elm and Main afterwards. They make these bear claws that are just incredible—flaky pastry, real almond paste inside, and sliced almonds on top. Not like the ones you get at the supermarket that are just dry bread with sugar glaze. You really have to get there before 9:00 AM though, or they sell out. The baker, his name is Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He used to be a boxer in his youth, can you believe it? Got a nose that looks like it's been broken three times, but he makes the most delicate pastries. \n\nAnyway, I haven't been to the walking group, and I haven't been to my book club either. I haven't even touched the garden. The weeds are probably taking over the tomatoes by now. I've even managed to upset some of my friends over at the senior center because, well, I guess everything has become about the memorial display lately. But I just can't focus on anything else until this is finished. It's too important.\n\nDoctor: Can anything convince you that the warnings you hear in the ballads might not be real or meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no. I mean, how could anything convince me otherwise? I know it sounds odd, I really do. I know my wife thinks I've lost the plot, she tells me I'm reading way too much into it. But I'm certain. Those old ballads my brother kept, they aren't just songs. They've got actual warnings tucked right into them, just for me. I know what I heard in those words. \n\nMy brother, he was always collecting things like that. Records, old stamps, coins. He had this whole album of wheat pennies. Do you remember those? They stopped making them in the fifties, I think. He had one from 1943 that was made of steel instead of copper because of the war. They needed the copper for ammunition, you see. It's fascinating, really, how war changes everything, even the money in your pocket. I remember my mother talking about ration books for sugar and butter. She used to save the bacon grease in a little tin can on the stove to use for cooking because you couldn't just go out and buy cooking oil whenever you wanted. People were so resourceful back then. Now, if the Wi-Fi goes out for ten minutes, people act like the world is ending. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were saying... nothing's going to convince me otherwise. I hear what I hear, and the warnings are real.\n\nDoctor: What do you think will happen if you don't follow the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, if I don't listen to them... I don't even want to find out. I know I keep repeating myself, and I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and I can't just ignore a warning like that. \n\nMy brother, he took such good care of those records. He used to play them on this massive wooden console stereo. It was a beautiful piece of furniture, really, took up half the living room wall. It had a radio built right into it, too, with those warm vacuum tubes that took a minute to heat up before the sound would come through. We used to sit around it and listen to the baseball games on Sunday afternoons. The announcers back then, they had such smooth voices. You could just close your eyes and perfectly picture the green grass of the outfield. I went to a game at Fenway Park once, back in... oh, it must have been '78 or '79. We had hot dogs that actually snapped when you bit into them. Best hot dog I ever had. They put celery salt on them, which sounds strange, but it really brings out the flavor. I actually bought a little bottle of celery salt at the grocer last week, but my wife hid it in the back of the pantry because she says it's too much sodium for my blood pressure. \n\nShe's always getting on my case about something these days. Just like when my wife says I’m reading too much into it with the records. She just doesn't get it. But I still know what I heard in those words. So, to answer you, I just can't risk finding out what happens if I don't listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else could hear these warnings if they listened to the ballads in the same way you do?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think they could. I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I'm certain those old ballads my brother kept aren't just songs. They've got warnings tucked in them specifically for me. So if someone else sat down and listened, they'd probably just hear the music. \n\nMy brother had this one record... it was a blues album, from the Delta. The vinyl was so thick and heavy, not like the flimsy ones they made in the eighties. You had to use a special needle for the 78s, you know. If you used the wrong needle, it would just tear the grooves right up. I remember going to the hardware store with my dad to buy replacement needles. The hardware store back then was amazing—it smelled like sawdust and motor oil, and they had these big wooden bins full of loose nails and screws. You didn't buy them in those little plastic blister packs like you do now. You'd scoop out what you needed, weigh them on a scale, and put them in a brown paper bag. The man who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he wore this heavy canvas apron with a hundred pockets. He always had a pencil tucked behind his ear. I try to keep a pencil behind my ear when I'm working on the memorial display, but it always falls out. Maybe my ears are the wrong shape for it. \n\nBut anyway, when my wife says I'm reading too much into the records and that anyone else would just hear regular singing, I just let her talk. Because I still know what I heard in those words. It's meant for me, not for anyone else who might put the needle on the record.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would happen to you if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, like I think I said a minute ago... I just don't know exactly what would happen, but it wouldn't be good. It's like ignoring a siren. You know, they used to test the civil defense sirens every first Tuesday of the month at noon where I grew up. Loudest thing you ever heard. We used to have to do those duck-and-cover drills in elementary school whenever they went off. We’d all crawl under our desks. We had these heavy oak desks—they still had the little holes for inkwells in the top, even though we all used ballpoint pens by then. Bic pens, usually, with the blue caps you could chew on. The teachers made us tuck our heads right down between our knees. Like a piece of wood was going to stop a nuclear bomb! My buddy Tommy, he used to try and tie my shoelaces together while we were huddled under there. He ended up moving to Chicago, became an accountant or something. I haven't seen him since 1982. \n\nBut anyway, ignoring a warning... you just don't do it. I know it sounds odd, I really do. And when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just let her complain, because I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. I still know what I heard in those words. So I just can't ignore them, whatever the consequences might be.\n\nDoctor: What do you think would be the worst thing that could happen if you ignored these warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, the absolute worst thing... I suppose the worst thing would be that I wouldn't be prepared for whatever is coming. Like I was saying, I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, it's like when they tell you a storm is coming on the radio. My father used to have this big Zenith trans-oceanic shortwave radio. It had this incredible dial with all the world cities listed on it—London, Paris, Tokyo. He’d sit up late at night twisting that big tuning knob, trying to catch a signal bouncing off the ionosphere. The static would just crackle and hiss, and then suddenly, clear as a bell, you'd hear a man speaking German or a music station from Havana. It felt like magic, pulling voices out of the thin air like that. We had this huge antenna strung up in the backyard between the chimney and the old oak tree. The squirrels used to run right across it. We had a lot of gray squirrels back then, but now all I see are those little red ones. They're mean, the red ones. They chased all the blue jays away from the bird feeder I put up last spring. I bought the expensive seed, too, the kind with the black oil sunflower seeds, but the squirrels just gorge themselves on it. \n\nSo, when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, about the records, I mean... I just ignore her. Because I still know what I heard in those words. If I ignore the warnings, it's on my own head. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: Well, doubt them? I mean, I know it sounds odd, I really do, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. So, no, I don't think anything could make me doubt them. \n\nMy brother, he used to be so careful with his things. He had this whole system for organizing his records, alphabetically by the artist's last name, and then chronologically by release date. He kept them in these wooden milk crates he painted green. You don't see wooden milk crates anymore. Everything is plastic now. I remember when milk used to get delivered to the house in glass bottles. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, drove this white truck with no doors on the sides so he could just hop in and out. In the winter, the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze and push the little paper cap right up into the air. We’d fight over who got to eat that frozen cream. It tasted like vanilla ice cream, almost. My mother used to save those little paper caps and use them for crafts. We made a whole Christmas garland out of them one year, painted them silver and strung them up over the fireplace. The fireplace always smoked, though. We had to keep the flue wide open and even then, the living room would smell like a campfire for days. \n\nBut getting back to the records... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I just have to tune her out. I still know what I heard in those words. The warnings are there, clear as day, and I'd be a fool to start doubting them now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the warnings you hear in the ballads are just a product of your own mind, rather than actual messages meant for you?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I know I keep saying this, and I know exactly how odd it sounds to you. My wife tells me the exact same thing you're asking—she says it's just my mind playing tricks, that I’m reading way too much into it. But I’m absolutely certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just regular songs. There are warnings tucked right in there for me. I still know what I heard in those words, and it's not my imagination. \n\nSpeaking of imagination, my grandfather used to have the wildest imagination of anyone I ever met. We used to sit out on his front porch in the evenings. He had this heavy wooden rocking chair that squeaked every single time he leaned back. He'd smoke a pipe, usually a cherry blend tobacco. You could smell that sweet cherry smoke halfway down the block before you even got to his house. Anyway, he used to tell us these tall tales, swore up and down he fought a black bear with his bare hands up in the UP—the Upper Peninsula, in Michigan. We believed him for years, right up until my grandmother finally told us he'd never even been north of Saginaw. She used to make the best cherry pie, my grandmother. She picked the tart cherries right off the tree in their side yard. You had to be careful eating it, though, because she'd always miss a few pits. You had to spit them out into a little paper napkin. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... it's not a product of my mind. I know what I heard in those records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still hear these warnings if a psychiatrist told you they aren't really there?\n\nPatient: Well, even if a psychiatrist told me they aren't there... I mean, no offense to you, Doc, but I know it sounds odd, I really do. But I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked in them for me. \n\nYou know, psychiatrists... my neighbor, Phil, he went to a psychiatrist once back in the seventies. He had this terrible fear of driving over bridges. Amaxophobia, I think they call it, or maybe gephyrophobia. Anyway, Phil drove this beautiful 1969 Chevy Impala. It was a two-door hardtop, cherry red with a white vinyl roof. He kept it immaculate. Every Sunday morning, he'd be out in the driveway with a bucket of soapy water and a chamois cloth. He even polished the chrome bumpers until you could see your reflection in them. But if he had to cross the river to get to the hardware store on the other side of town, he'd drive ten miles out of his way just to take the road that went around the water instead of over the bridge. The bridge was one of those old steel truss ones, painted a faded green, and it rattled whenever a heavy truck went over it. The city finally tore it down a few years ago and put up a concrete one. It's much quieter now, but it doesn't have the same character. \n\nBut like I was saying... when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, or if a doctor tells me it's not real, I just... I still know what I heard in those words. I can't just unhear it because someone else tells me I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
